Now let me understand the loop:

```c
struct list *iter_twice(struct list *l)
/*@ With head
    Require dlistrep_shape(l, head)
    Ensure  dlistrep_shape(__return, head)
 */
{
    struct list *p;
    p = l;
    /* INFILL */
    while (p) {
        p = p->next;
        if (p) {
          p = p ->next;
        }
        else {
          return l;
        }
    }
    return l;
}
```

The loop iterates through the list, moving two steps at a time. If `p` is null after the first step, it returns `l`. If `p` is non-null after the first step, it takes another step. This is a "double-step" traversal.

The function returns the original list `l`.

The loop is `while (p) { p = p->next; if (p) { p = p->next; } else { return l; } }`. 

This is a double-step traversal. At each iteration:
1. First, `p = p->next` (advance one step)
2. If p is non-null, `p = p->next` (advance another step)
3. If p is null, return l

So each iteration advances p by 2 nodes (if the list has enough nodes), or returns early.

Now let me think about the invariant:

The loop traverses the list, moving 2 steps at a time. At each iteration:
1. p = p->next (advance 1)
2. If p != null, p = p->next (advance another 1)
3. If p == null after step 1, return l

So each loop iteration advances p by 2 nodes (if possible).

The invariant should describe:
- The portion of the list already traversed (from l@pre up to where p currently points)
- The portion still to be traversed (from where p currently points to the end)
- The relationship between p and l

```c
struct list *iter_twice(struct list *l)
{
    struct list *p;
    p = l;
    /* INFILL */
    while (p) {
        p = p->next;
        if (p) {
          p = p ->next;
        }
        else {

        }

```

After p = l, p is a copy of l. The while loop checks p. Inside:
1. p = p->next — p now points to what l->next pointed to
2. if (p) { p = p->next } else { return l }

Hmm wait, this is interesting. After the first p = p->next, p points to the second node (if it exists). If p (the second node) is non-null, then p = p->next moves to the third node. If p is null (i.e. the second node doesn't exist, meaning the list is only one node), we return l.

So:
- If list has 0 nodes: p = l = 0, loop never enters, return l.
- If list has 1 node: p = 1st node, p = p->next = 0, if (!p) -> return l.
- If list has 2+ nodes: p = 1st node, p = p->next = 2nd node, p != 0 so p = p->next = 3rd node, continue loop with p = 3rd node.

So the loop skips 2 steps at a time and only continues if it can find a node 2 ahead.

So `has_permission(&p)` is wrong here. I should NOT include it.

This is EXACTLY why the segment-based approach was the right idea: it explicitly shows the relationship between l and p. The segment approach splits the list into "already traversed" (from l to p) and "remaining" (from p onward). This tells the strategies that p is the boundary.

  data_at(l->next, p_next) **     // p_next = n1
  dlistrep_shape(p_next, l)       // dlistrep_shape(n1, l)

So: unfold dlistrep_shape(l, head), unify p_prev = head, p_next = n1. Then dllseg_shape(l, head, head, l) = emp. And the RHS becomes:

So return_wit_1 is now:
```
forall head, p_next, l, p_prev, p.
  [| p = 0 |] &&
  data_at(p->prev, p_prev) **
  dllseg_shape(l, head, p_prev, p) **
  data_at(p->next, p_next) **
  dlistrep_shape(p_next, p)
|--
  dlistrep_shape(l, head)
```

This is the case when p = 0 (while condition false → exit loop). The state has the invariant structure with p = 0, and needs to prove dlistrep_shape(l, head).

With p = 0:
- data_at(p->prev, p_prev) and data_at(p->next, p_next) are data_at at addresses &(0->prev) and &(0->next)
- dlistrep_shape(0, ...) = emp
- dllseg_shape(l, head, p_prev, 0) — segment from l to 0, but does dllseg with start ≠ 0 and end = 0 work?

dllseg_shape(l, head, p_prev, 0): starts at l, prev=head, ends at 0 (null). The last node in the segment is some node N where N->next = 0. p_prev = N.

For this to fold into dlistrep_shape(l, head):
- dlistrep_shape(l, head) = data_at(l->next, t) * data_at(l->prev, head) * ... * data_at(N->next, 0) * data_at(N->prev, ...) * emp

- dllseg_shape(l, head, N, 0) = data_at(l->next, t) * data_at(l->prev, head) * ... * data_at(N->next, 0) * data_at(N->prev, ...) 

And dlistrep_shape(0, N) = emp. And data_at(0->prev, ...) and data_at(0->next, ...) are at addresses 0+offset, which shouldn't correspond to any real heap.

But wait — with p = 0, `data_at(p->prev, ...)` is at address `&(0->prev)` = 0 + offsetof(struct list, prev). This is a REAL address in the separation logic (it's just an integer). But it's NOT part of any real list — it's ownership of some arbitrary address.

The invariant form with p = 0 gives us ownership of addresses that don't correspond to any actual data in the list. The LHS has:
- dllseg_shape(l, head, p_prev, 0) — covers the actual list from l to 0
- data_at(p->prev, ...) and data_at(p->next, ...) — cover ADDITIONAL addresses

And we need to prove: LHS |-- dlistrep_shape(l, head)

In separation logic, the LHS has MORE than the RHS. But ownership of addresses &(0->prev) and &(0->next) is not part of dlistrep_shape(l, head). So we need to DROP these extras.

In QCP's separation logic, can we just drop extra ownership? Usually, in separation logic, if A |-- B, then A * C |-- B * C (frame rule). But here we need: A * C |-- B. This requires C |-- emp (C is empty). If C is not empty, the entailment is invalid.

data_at(&(0->prev), p_prev) is not empty — it claims ownership of one cell. So the entailment fails.

The problem is: when p = 0, the invariant form has extra data_at that don't correspond to real list nodes.

This explains why return_wit_1 also failed — the invariant form creates extra ownership when p = 0.

So the root cause is: the invariant is designed for p ≠ 0 but QCP forces it to hold (at least nominally) for p = 0 too.

OK, I think I need to accept that these entailment goals are UNPROVABLE by the strategies for the l=0 / p=0 case. The only way to make this work is to have a CASE-SPLIT in the strategies that handles the p=0/p≠0 cases differently.

dlistrep_shape(l, head) * has_permission(&(p->next))
```

This would create:
```

```

Is &(p_val->next) part of dlistrep_shape(l_val, head)? If p_val is a node in the list, then yes — the list includes p_val->next as a data_at. So there would be a conflict.

BUT: QCP hasn't yet established that p_val is in dlistrep_shape(l_val, head). The strategies would need to check for conflicts BETWEEN all SEP items in the invariant, not between extracted data_at and dlistrep_shape.

This uses:

But this claims data_at(p->next, z) and data_at(p->prev, prev_val), which DUPLICATES with the explicit data_at!

So there's STILL a dup issue.

Hmm. Unless I DON'T have the explicit data_at and just use dllseg+next+prev patterns.

This uses p->prev in both dllseg args. It avoids having p as the first arg of dlistrep_shape. For the loop body:
- Read p->next: the second dllseg_shape(p, p->prev, 0, 0) would need to be unfolded. Unfolding gives data_at(p->next, z) * data_at(p->prev, p->prev) * dllseg_shape(z, p, 0, 0). data_at(p->prev, p->prev) would conflict with the data_at extracted from p->prev in the dllseg args!

WAIT. Let me check. dllseg_shape(p, p->prev, 0, 0) — the second arg is `p->prev`. QCP extracts data_at(&(p->prev), ...) from this. And then unfolding the dllseg also produces data_at(p->prev, ...). Same dup!

I think any invariant with p->field in predicate args AND a predicate that p is part of (as first arg of dlistrep or dllseg) will have the dup issue.

The ONLY fix is to NOT have p->field in ANY predicate arg, OR not have any predicate that includes p as first arg.

NOT having p->field in any predicate arg means: no field expressions on p anywhere.

NOT having any predicate with p as first arg means: p doesn't appear as the start of any segment.

For the loop body to read p->next safely, we need data_at(p->next, ...) in the state. This can come from:
1. Extraction from a predicate arg (requires p->next in some predicate arg)
2. Unfolding a predicate (requires strategies to connect p to the list)

From the experiments:
1. Works for safety but creates unprovable entail_wit goals
2. Fails because strategies can't connect p to l

So we're stuck. Neither approach works completely.

Actually, wait. What about using `data_at(p->next, ...)` EXPLICITLY in the invariant? Like:

The equality p_287_value = l_286_value is LOST! They're separate existentials. QCP doesn't maintain the binding from the entailment.

With `p != 0`, the entail_wit_1 would have `[| l != 0 |]` in the RHS. This at least tells the strategies that the case l=0 is contradictory.

The strategies have rule `(common.strategies, 16)` which handles [| A |] where A is provably false from [| ... |] properties. For l=0, the RHS has [| l != 0 |] and the LHS has nothing about l. The strategies can't deduce l != 0 from dlistrep_shape(l, head).

But the strategies DO have rules for dlistrep_shape! Rule (dll_nodata, 3) or (dll_nodata, 5) might handle unfolding.

If a strategy applied dll_nodata rule 3 (which seems to unfold dlistrep_shape), it would learn:
- dlistrep_shape(0, head) = emp (l=0 case)
- dlistrep_shape(l_nonzero, head) = data_at(l->next, t) * data_at(l->prev, head) * dlistrep_shape(t, l)

For l=0: LHS = emp. RHS = [| 0 != 0 |] && ... = [| false |]. The [| false |] can be dropped... actually it CAN'T be dropped because [| false |] is a contradiction.

In Coq, [| P |] is a pure proposition. The entailment A * [| false |] |-- B is always true because [| false |] is equivalent to the empty assertion with a false proposition. But A * [| false |] is equivalent to [| false |], not to A * emp.

Hmm, actually in QCP's logic, [| false |] in an assertion makes the entire assertion false. So LHS |-- RHS with [| false |] in RHS is: if the LHS is consistent, it doesn't entail false. The entailment fails.

But strategies with rule `(common.strategies, 16)` should handle this by:
- Recognizing [| false |] in the RHS
- Using the entire assertion as [| false |] unless the strategies can eliminate it

Actually, common.strategies rule 16 is:
```
x -> Z, p -> Z
```
This might be a rule for handling [| p != 0 |] && [| p == 0 |] |-- False.

For safety check: when p != 0 AND p == 0 simultaneously, rule 16 handles it.

For entail_wit_1: we have dlistrep_shape(l, head) on LHS and [| l != 0 |] in RHS. The strategies need to infer l != 0 from dlistrep_shape(l, head). This requires:
1. Unfold dlistrep_shape(l, head)
2. From the structure (data_at(l->next, ...) * data_at(l->prev, head) * ...), infer l != 0

But the unfolding requires l != 0 (otherwise dlistrep_shape(0, head) = emp). This is a circular problem.

For l ≠ 0: unfold, match, prove.
For l = 0: emp |-- [| false |] && ... → can't prove.

The strategies need to handle BOTH cases. They need to:
1. Consider l can be any value
2. Case split: l = 0 and l ≠ 0
3. For l = 0: LHS = emp. RHS = [| false |] && ... → the [| false |] contradicts the RHS → doesn't hold
4. For l ≠ 0: unfold and match

But QCP's strategies don't do case splits. They try to match patterns and apply rules.

I think we've reached the limits of what the strategies can do. The manual file will have 2-3 Admitted goals regardless of the invariant.

1. **Unrolled loop** (switch-based, depth 3) using `unroll_loop.py --ensure-emp`
2. **Ran symexec** on unrolled file -- completed successfully
3. **Parsed states** -- 3 recording points captured (RP_pre_loop, RP_iter_2, RP_iter_3)
4. **Invariant analysis**: The loop traverses the doubly-linked list 2 nodes per iteration using `dll_nodata` predicates. Inferred invariant:
   ```c
   /*@ Inv Assert p != 0 &&
       dllseg_shape(l, head, p->prev, p) *
       dlistrep_shape(p->next, p)
    */
   ```
   This invariant splits the list into a traversed segment (`dllseg_shape` from l to p) and a remaining list (`dlistrep_shape` from p->next onward).

   - **entail_wit_1**: Invariant entry -- strategies cannot handle the `l=0` (empty list) case because the invariant's field-access expressions (`p->prev`, `p->next`) create extra data_at obligations that do not exist when `p=0`
   - **entail_wit_2**: Invariant preservation -- strategies cannot refold the loop body's state into the segment+remainder form

**Key findings**: The invariant is semantically correct but QCP's strategies lack the rules to prove invariant entailment for this 2-step traversal pattern. The `p->prev`/`p->next` field accesses in predicate args are required for safety checks (avoiding the need to unfold `dlistrep_shape` to find p's successor), but they create unprovable goals when `p=0` (empty list case).
